COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-2044
DATE: 2016-09-29T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

Restricting one's right to the use of their own stem cells is an infringement of the right of control over one's own body. Stem cells are a valuable medical tool and it is important that people are allowed unrestricted access to their use. Under no other circumstances are substances removed from the human body considered to be a drug, so it makes no sense that stem cells should be classified differently. There is no reason that United States citizens should not be able to use their own stem cells for medical purposes if they wish to. Please do not restrict this basic right.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-0389
DATE: 2016-09-29T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

To whom it may concern: My name is Ken Woods and I had SVF stem cell therapy 3 years ago. I did this based on much research and little other alternatives. I saw the positive reactions of many years in the veterinary side and many positive results in the human patients. I like the animal side as there is no placebo effect. I was bone on bone and in line for a knee replacement at 40 years of age. This was not a good option for me as I felt the medical industry is advancing so fast there would be much better options if I could get 5 years down the road. This has proven to be true. I am feeling great and very active at 44. I know FDA has many concerns but their main one should be safety. The thousands of procedures performed in the US and outside would prove that it was safe. Stem cells are healing agents within your body and they are just being put in places that they can do the most good in a same day surgical procedure. This should fall within the guidelines of a surgical procedure and should not be regulated by the FDA but by state medical boards. Physicians and patients wouldn't be flocking to the procedure if it wasn't safe and effective. Leave the choice between the patient and physician. Patients are already going outside the US for treatment. Please keep them here and not make the regulations so arduous and costly that they go elsewhere. Medicine in the United States is the safest place to receive it, please don't force people outside the country to get cutting edge treatments. Stem cells from one's own body are not a drug and are safe. Patients must be informed and not oversold the procedure. Making sure there aren't overstated claims and procedures are done in a sterile environment is a within the jurisdiction of the FDA. FDA should encourage state medical boards to make sure they are operating under an approved IRB and are gathering data from patients to help get the procedure approved. Thank you for taking the time to view my comments. I continue to tell people to look at stem cell therapy as an alternative to very invasive surgery if it makes sense. Please allow them to stay in the United States for this procedure.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-2538
DATE: 2016-09-30T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

As Americans, and as humans, we are guaranteed certain inalienable rights, some of which were famously enumerated by Thomas Jefferson in the Declaration of Independence: those rights are of liberty, justice, and the pursuit of happiness. Absent from that document is mention of another intrinsic right of any human being: that to their own bodies. Someone's body is unquestionably their right; and they have every right to do as they see fit. The simple fact of the matter is that by classifying one's own body in the form of their cells as a drug, we are taking away their right to their body. We must therefore declassify stem cells as drugs and put the power of control back into whoever's body the cells came from.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-2616
DATE: 2016-09-30T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

The right to access and utilize the cells of one's own body for medical care, and to seek and obtain assistance in that endeavor from medical professionals, should be inviolable. Please do not allow the interests and profits of industry to come before, interfere with, or in any way impede the progress and implementation of scientific and medical advancements in service to the care of mankind, and compassionate and skillful response to the illnesses and diseases thereof. Please also recognize that the individual's right itself to make healthcare decisions and to access care, freely and with support, is as vital to the physical and psychological health of the population as any newly developed medicine or therapeutic technique. Sickness, aging, accident, and death are both inevitable and unpredictable, rendering their utter commodification not only sad and foolish, but shameful and unethical. Our administrative decisions and regulations should reflect as nuanced an understanding of this point as is possible, along with full knowledge of the fact that we can pragmatically restructure and reprioritize each and every one of our habitual and cultural systems.

This is akin to saying that to digest food, one should have a pharmaceutical license. (As this process involves the conversion of organic matter to various compounds, some of which, if synthesized, would be scheduled substances.) If one does not own the products of their own body, this implies that one's body is owned by another person, organization, or corporation. Unless a person is to be considered property, the products of that person's body should be able to be used at that individual's discretion. Perhaps the preceeding is somewhat hyperbolic, but to an extent this is what the issue breaks down to. Stem cell treatments have enormous potential, and should not have artificial constraints placed upon their usage. Either we continue to try to improve human health and well-being, or we allow people to die because they have had their treatment options constrained in favor of corporate interests.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-3520
DATE: 2016-09-30T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

My cells are MY cells. They are not a drug, they are a part of my body. No one, including the FDA, has any right to prevent me and my doctor from using my body in any way which I deem acceptable. Control over one's own body is supposed to be an inalienable right. Please don't take another one of my rights away. Stem cell treatments can be used to cure or mitigate an exceptionally large number of conditions, and it strikes me as odd that when taken from the patient's own body they are classified as a drug. In essence, it is no different than performing a skin graft by taking flesh from one region of a patient's body to treat a severe burn. In the same fashion, stem cells are taken from the body in order to treat a condition. If a skin graft is not considered a drug, how can stem cells be? Right now, stem cells seem to be our 'miracle treatment', almost a panacea - they can repair brain damage, regrow cochlear hair to restore hearing, heal flesh wounds, regrow teeth, and even cure infertility - and that is only with the research we have performed using stem cells so far. There is no telling how far these cells can advance our medical sciences, and it seems that if they are taken from a consenting, informed patient to be used upon themselves, there are very few downsides to their application. I am of the firm belief that these cells should not be classified as drugs, and should instead be labeled as a medical procedure no different from a skin graft in practice - but much more effective in producing results, as stated above. You might as well start taking my white blood cells away so that I can buy new ones from billion dollar pharmaceutical companies.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-2558
DATE: 2016-09-30T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

The use of any materials from one's own body for the maintenance of one's own health is ethically analogous to grafting of skin, tendon, bone, and other tissue from one location to another. This provides no economic mechanism for negative externalities, and unlike the organ trade and prostitution, carries no potential to propagate or introduce additional social injustice and shows no clear path to abuse. This issue is ethically clear-cut; in fact, the question of regulation of the use of one's own stem cells will serve as a very precise metric for the degree to which pharmaceutical companies are able to influence regulation through sheer financial clout. Maybe we should all be past hoping to have our faith in US pharmaceutical regulation restored, given the recent shkrelification of Daraprim and the Epi-pen, but some of us are still hanging on, so try to give us a decision that will let you sleep at night.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-3122
DATE: 2016-09-30T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

To Whom It May Concern: The FDA's regulations and guidelines regarding the safety and effectiveness of drug products are a big part of what keeps Americans confident in the medicine they need to continue being happy and productful. These regulations are required, because it's been seen in the past what happens when producers of medicine are unregulated: cost-cutting measures in safety and efficacy, a conflict of interest which put the general public at risk. However, consider the following: The human body is a producer of medicine. A self-regulating one, at that. And it does not engage in potentially disastrous cost-cutting measures, because it garners little benefit from jeopardizing its own survival. With this model of thought in place, medicine produced by one's own body, for one's own body, is as safe and effective as one's body is physically capable of producing. There is no conflict of interest. Under this reasoning, I feel that human cellular products that are produced and consumed by the same individual require no federal regulation. The medicine is tailor-made for the individual, and is no more harmful than the best-produced medicines of the safest drug companies. One could make an argument against using the drug products from one individual on others (due to hidden incompatibilities and other such complications; see: blood transfusions and blood typing), but my body's medicines should be my own to do with to myself as I will. A contemporary example of this is stem cell therapy: if my body has stem cells, then they should be usable by myself in whatever procedures are deemed safe and medically relevant by a medical health professional, and myself. No further regulation required, unless there is a demonstrable safety risk - and currently, to my knowledge, there is not. Thank you for your consideration.

Why should I have to leave the U.S. because of the FDA's decision to "fix" something that isn't broken? These regulations will do far more harm than good. I am now living a far more productive life. I have had no incidents of pneumonia or bronchitis (which I used to have all the time), I can go shopping, climb stairs, cook, care for my pets, go bird watching, none of which I could do before stem cell therapy. My lung disease had progressed to the point I was unable to walk from the parking lot to the inside of a restaurant. It took me 20 minutes to climb the stairs to the upstairs level of my home. I was miserable. It truly was no life and I felt like I was a burden to my husband and my family. Why does the FDA want to deny patients the right to use their own body parts when many are getting the same results I've had? I know about other patients because the patient community is one of sharing and caring. The decision to use one's own body tissue is a personal one. The FDA should not be interfering in something that it is quite obvious they do not truly understand. The FDA needs to withdraw the four HCT/P guidance documents and allow patients to continue to access stem cell therapy from responsible doctors that use our own tissue.

================================================================================

Bodily autonomy is an human right. What one does with one's own body is nobody's business but their own; this applies to everything from breast milk to blood to semen to skin to organs. No one can force a person to give blood, even if their doing so would save lives. No one can force a person to donate skin or organs; we don't take organs from a dead body if the person it belonged to didn't want us to. It goes the other way as well. No one can deprive me of my blood, prevent me from saving my cut hair and doing whatever I want with it, or in any way prevent me from using a part of my body how I see fit as long as its use relates only to me. I see no reason why I should not be able to save my own stem cells for my personal use. Enforcing restrictions on how any person can use their own stem cells for their own purposes is a blatant violation of human rights no better than preventing mothers from saving their breast milk for later use. It is no better than denying a person the right to use their own blood to save their own life. Denying people the freedom to use their own bodies to their own benefit both violates human rights, and takes lives. The only reason that this travesty isn't considered murder is that the definition of murder includes "unlawful."

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-5707
DATE: 2016-10-03T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

I simply think that it's erroneous to classify an individual's stem cells as a "drug" if they are being used to treat one's own body. If used in another person, this may be sensible (although we do not classify donor organs/tissues as "drugs" and it's essentially the same idea), but not if it's from your body, for your body. Unless if this serves an important public safety role, which I personally do not see it as doing, it seems to be unnecessary. I expect that the benefits from disposing of this regulation would vastly outweigh the potential costs.

COMMENT ID: FDA-2015-D-3719-6210
DATE: 2016-10-03T04:00:00Z
================================================================================

I am against the classification of one's own stem cells as a drug. Such an idea serves no good to patients and introduces many bureaucratic hurdles that seem to have no purpose - it is part of one's own body and not a drug created by any pharmaceutical company. It also sets a terrible precedent, would one's skin then be a drug given that it is removed and grafted to other parts of one's body in the event of major burns or other trauma? What if blood were classified as a drug - many could die due to introduced regulations acting as an obstacle to blood transfusions. Anything that comes out of one's body should not be classified as a drug, period. We are not talking about synthetic stem cells created by a pharmaceutical company, we are talking about something analogous to skin grafts and blood transfusions.

================================================================================
